Polynomial normalization and inverse iteration are classical tools for attracting basins, notably in Rosay–Rudin’s work <a id="pm-cite-90"></a>[[RosayRudin1988](#ref-RosayRudin1988), Appendix]; Chau–Tam brought this strategy into Kähler uniformization <a id="pm-cite-91"></a>[[ChauTamComplex2006](#ref-ChauTamComplex2006), [ChauTamStein](#ref-ChauTamStein), [ChauTamSimply](#ref-ChauTamSimply)]. The estimates proved here address the nonuniform contraction permitted by Proposition <a href="#prop:ricci-windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:ricci-windows">23</a>. For sequences of holomorphic automorphisms of $\mathbb C^n$, $n\ge2$, fixing zero and satisfying $A|z|\le|f_j(z)|\le B|z|$ on one fixed ball about zero, with $0<A<B<1$ independent of $j$, Bera–Verma prove that the attracting basin is biholomorphic to $\mathbb C^n$ <a id="pm-cite-92"></a>[[BeraVerma2024](#ref-BeraVerma2024), Theorem 1.1]. The chart transitions here require the variable-rate estimates below. In particular, we use only $$q\text{ nondecreasing},\qquad q\ge c>0,\qquad
 \liminf_{s\to\infty}\frac{q(s)}s\le2,\qquad
 \lambda_{\min}(t\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}})(o)\ge b_0q(a_0s),
 \label{eq:dyn:rate-input}$$ where $0<a_0<1$ and $b_0>0$ are fixed.

We continue to use $J_d$ and the fixed coefficient norms introduced in Section <a href="#sec:charts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:charts">7</a>. All maps and jets in this Section fix zero. A coefficient bound $\exp(o(s_k))$ means an upper bound $\exp(\eta_k s_k)$ with $\eta_k\to0$; its rate of convergence may depend on parameters already fixed. No assertion of uniformity as those parameters vary is intended. For polynomial automorphisms put $G_{k,l}=G_{l-1}\circ\cdots\circ G_k$ and $G_{k,k}=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$.

<a id="stmt-prop-polynomial-models"></a>
<div id="prop:polynomial-models" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:dyn:grid-size"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn:jet-conjugacy"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn:coefficient-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn:center-inverse"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn:forward-degree"></a>
**Proposition 27** (Polynomial models). *Fix an integer $d\ge2$ and a macro width $0<h<1$. After starting at a sufficiently late macro endpoint, there is a grid $s_k\to\infty$ with macro endpoints $S_j=e^{hj}$ and the following properties. Each macro interval is either one bad step or is divided into regular steps of length comparable to $\sqrt{S_j}$. Thus, eventually, $$c_h\sqrt{s_k}\le\Delta s_k:=s_{k+1}-s_k
 \le(e^h-1)s_k.
 \label{eq:dyn:grid-size}$$ For $F_k=F_{s_k,s_{k+1}}$ there are polynomial local coordinate changes $H_k$ and polynomial automorphisms $G_k$ of $\mathbb C^n$ satisfying $$J_d(H_{k+1}\circ F_k)=J_d(G_k\circ H_k).
 \label{eq:dyn:jet-conjugacy}$$ The degrees of $H_k,G_k,G_k^{-1}$ are bounded independently of $k$. The coefficients of $H_k$ and its inverse jet are $\exp(o(s_k))$; $H_k'(0)$ and its inverse have polynomial bounds in $s_k$. For a constant $C_d$ depending only on the fixed jet order and dimension, $$\begin{aligned}
 \lVert G_k\rVert_{\mathrm{coeff}}+\lVert G_k^{-1}\rVert_{\mathrm{coeff}}
 &\le \exp\bigl(C_d\Delta s_k+o(s_k)\bigr),
 \label{eq:dyn:coefficient-bound}\\
 \lVert G_k'(0)^{-1}\rVert
 &\le s_k^{C}\exp(\Delta s_k/2).
 \label{eq:dyn:center-inverse}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, the coefficient $C_d$ of $\Delta s_k$ is independent of $h$. Finally, for some finite $D$, $$\deg G_{0,k}\le D s_k
 \quad\text{on an unbounded subsequence of macro endpoints.}
 \label{eq:dyn:forward-degree}$$*

</div>

First we normalize the Jacobian jets coherently along the grid. On regular steps we then construct polynomial models whose nonlinear terms and intervening linear changes preserve bounds for the degree of each component. On bad steps, realization of the full normalized jet costs a fixed degree factor. The last part of the proof charges these factors to growth of $q$ and obtains <a href="#eq:dyn:forward-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:forward-degree">[eq:dyn:forward-degree]</a>. The distinction between the constant $C_d$ in <a href="#eq:dyn:coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:coefficient-bound">[eq:dyn:coefficient-bound]</a> and constants hidden in $o(s_k)$ will be used in Section <a href="#sec:uniformization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:uniformization">9</a>.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Coherent volume jets

<a id="eq:dyn:transition-linear"></a>
On the uniform transition ball, the germs $F_k$ are nonsingular and have uniformly bounded derivatives of each fixed order on a smaller ball. Their center derivatives are contractions, and chart normalization gives $$\lvert\det F_k'(0)\rvert=e^{-\Delta s_k/2},\qquad
 \lVert F_k'(0)^{-1}\rVert\le e^{\Delta s_k/2}.
 \label{eq:dyn:transition-linear}$$ The second inequality follows because every singular value is at most one and their product is $e^{-\Delta s_k/2}$. Choose the holomorphic logarithm vanishing at zero of $$P_k(z)=\log\frac{\det F_k'(z)}{\det F_k'(0)}.$$ The upper bound on its real part is $C+\Delta s_k/2$. The coefficient estimate for a holomorphic function with bounded real part, applied on concentric balls and with $P_k(0)=0$, therefore gives $\lVert J_{d-1}P_k\rVert\le C_d(1+\Delta s_k)$.

The volume correction uses only the spacing bounds <a href="#eq:dyn:grid-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:grid-size">[eq:dyn:grid-size]</a>, so it can be constructed before the macro intervals are classified. On any such grid the jet series <a id="eq:dyn:volume-series"></a>$$\ell_k=\sum_{l\ge k}J_{d-1}
       \bigl(P_l\circ F_{s_k,s_l}\bigr)
 \label{eq:dyn:volume-series}$$ converges. Indeed Proposition <a href="#prop:shrinking-charts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shrinking-charts">26</a> bounds each coefficient of the summand by $$C_d(1+\Delta s_l)
       \min\{1,\exp(C_d s_k-\sigma s_l)\}.$$ The absence of a constant term in $P_l$ is essential here. For $s_l\le C'_d s_k$ the step lengths telescope and the number of steps is polynomial in $s_k$. Beyond that range the displayed exponential is summable, since the grid eventually has spacing at least one and $\Delta s_l\le(e^h-1)s_l$. Thus $\ell_k$ has polynomial coefficient bounds in $s_k$. The composition identity for transitions yields <a id="eq:dyn:volume-cocycle"></a>$$\ell_k=J_{d-1}\bigl(P_k+\ell_{k+1}\circ F_k\bigr).
 \label{eq:dyn:volume-cocycle}$$

Let $E_k=J_{d-1}\exp(\ell_k)$ and define a polynomial change $$A_k(z)=\left(z_1,\ldots,z_{n-1},
       \int_0^{z_n}E_k(z_1,\ldots,z_{n-1},\zeta)\,d\zeta\right).$$ It is tangent to the identity, has degree at most $d$, and satisfies $J_{d-1}\log\det A_k'=\ell_k$. Its coefficients and inverse jet have polynomial bounds in $s_k$. In the new coordinates the normalized logarithmic Jacobian jet of $A_{k+1}F_kA_k^{-1}$ is $$J_{d-1}\bigl(\ell_{k+1}\circ F_k+P_k-\ell_k\bigr)\circ A_k^{-1}=0.$$ This records the sign of the volume correction explicitly. We shall call a jet with Jacobian determinant equal, through degree $d-1$, to its center determinant a *constant-volume jet*.

<a id="stmt-lem-volume-jets"></a>
<div id="lem:volume-jets" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (Realization of constant-volume jets). *For fixed $n,d$, every invertible constant-volume $d$-jet fixing zero is the $d$-jet of a polynomial automorphism of $\mathbb C^n$. Its degree and inverse degree are bounded by a constant $D_d$. Its coefficients and inverse coefficients are bounded polynomially in the coefficients of the given jet and its inverse linear part. These bounds do not depend on the grid or on $h$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-volume-jets"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-volume-jets</summary>

*Proof.* Normalize the derivative to the identity. If a volume-preserving polynomial automorphism agrees with the required jet through degree $m-1$, the first discrepancy is a homogeneous vector polynomial $X_m$ of degree $m$ with $\operatorname{div}X_m=0$: this is the degree $m-1$ part of the Jacobian determinant condition.

<a id="eq:dyn:shear-fields"></a>
The space of such fields is spanned by $$X(z)=l(z)^m v,\qquad l\in(\mathbb C^n)^*,\quad v\in\mathbb C^n,\quad l(v)=0.
 \label{eq:dyn:shear-fields}$$ This is the divergence-free shear lemma of Andersén <a id="pm-cite-93"></a>[[AndersenVolume1990](#ref-AndersenVolume1990)]; see also <a id="pm-cite-94"></a>[[ForstnericLarusson](#ref-ForstnericLarusson), proof of Theorem 3.1]. We include the finite-dimensional proof to track the coefficient bounds. Pair a linear functional on homogeneous vector polynomials with $l^m v$. The result has the form $A(l)(v)$, where $A(l)$ is a covector-valued homogeneous polynomial of degree $m$. If the functional annihilates all fields in <a href="#eq:dyn:shear-fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:shear-fields">[eq:dyn:shear-fields]</a>, then $A(l)$ is proportional to $l$. The polynomial identities $l_iA_j(l)=l_jA_i(l)$ show that $A(l)=B(l)l$ for a homogeneous polynomial $B$ of degree $m-1$. Such functionals are exactly the image of the transpose of divergence, since $\operatorname{div}(l^m v)=m l(v)l^{m-1}$. Taking annihilators proves the spanning assertion. In dimension one the divergence kernel in these degrees is zero, so this argument also covers $n=1$.

Select a finite spanning list of the fields <a href="#eq:dyn:shear-fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:shear-fields">[eq:dyn:shear-fields]</a>, once for each $2\le m\le d$. The time-$c$ map of such a field is the polynomial shear $z\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,space=false,pdfliteral=direct,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto%
  \EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}%
}z+c l(z)^m v$, whose inverse has the minus sign; both have Jacobian determinant one. A fixed linear decomposition of $X_m$ therefore gives a finite composition of shears correcting degree $m$ without changing lower degrees. Induction through $m=d$, followed by the original linear map, proves realization. There are a fixed number of shears, and each operation on the truncated jets is polynomial in the preceding coefficients and the inverse linear part. This proves all the coefficient and degree bounds, including those for the inverse. Restoring the original linear map gives the realizing automorphism the determinant of that linear map; the correcting shears have determinant one. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### Rate order without commuting matrices

Consider one interval in logarithmic time, of length $T>0$. Singular-value decompositions in its start and end unitary tangent coordinates give derivative <a id="eq:dyn:diagonal-rates"></a>$$D=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(e^{-\mu_1T/2},\ldots,e^{-\mu_nT/2}),\qquad
 0\le\mu_1\le\cdots\le\mu_n,\qquad
 \sum_i\mu_i=\frac{\Delta s}{T}.
 \label{eq:dyn:diagonal-rates}$$ The volume changes $A_k$ do not change these linear parts.

<a id="eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order"></a>
We need an order statement for the rate forms, and not merely their ordered eigenvalues. Normalize the cometric at the start to $I$ in a fixed covector basis. In the diagonalized basis its value at the end is $\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(e^{\mu_iT})$. On the complex strip $0\le\Re z\le T$, the holomorphic covector with components $a_i e^{-\mu_i z/2}$ has squared norm $\lvert a\rvert^2$ on both boundary lines. Its squared norm is bounded and subharmonic by Theorem <a href="#thm:joint-positivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:joint-positivity">19</a>, applied with $t=t_{\mathrm{start}}e^{\Re z}$; equivalently the complex time variable there is shifted by $z/2$. The bounded subharmonic maximum principle on the strip gives the same upper bound inside. Consequently the cometric is bounded above by the flat exponential interpolation of its endpoint values. Differentiating this matrix inequality at the two ends gives $$(t\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}})_{\mathrm{start}}\le\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\mu_i),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\mu_i)\le(t\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}})_{\mathrm{end}},
 \label{eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order}$$ where each matrix is expressed in the corresponding endpoint unitary covector coordinates. The inequalities have opposite endpoint signs because the matrix difference vanishes at both ends and is nonnegative inside.

<a id="eq:dyn:loewner-jump"></a>
At a shared endpoint of two steps, let $U_k$ send old tangent components to new tangent components. It is unitary; a row covector $v$ in the new coordinates has old row $vU_k$. Combining the two inequalities in <a href="#eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order">[eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order]</a> gives $$\sum_l\mu_l^{\mathrm{old}}\lvert(vU_k)_l\rvert^2
 \le\sum_i\mu_i^{\mathrm{new}}\lvert v_i\rvert^2.
 \label{eq:dyn:loewner-jump}$$ This proof allows the instantaneous Ricci matrices to fail to commute. In particular, the sorted rates are nondecreasing from step to step.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### The macro grid and propagated weights

The positive weights below will bound ordinary polynomial degrees component by component. For a polynomial map $P=(P_1,\ldots,P_n):\mathbb C^n\to\mathbb C^n$ with $\deg P_l\le w_l$, one has $$\deg(P_1^{\beta_1}\cdots P_n^{\beta_n})
 \le\sum_l\beta_lw_l.$$ Thus a nonlinear map preserves these degree bounds if every monomial $z^\beta$ in component $i$ satisfies $\sum_l\beta_lw_l\le w_i$. A linear change between two weight systems preserves the bounds provided every nonzero entry sends an old coordinate to a new coordinate of at least its weight. We shall enforce both rules on regular steps. Bad steps may multiply degrees by a fixed factor; their defining rate growth will pay for that factor.

Put $Q_j=q(S_j)$ and choose an integer $N\ge1$ with $e^{-hN}\le a_0$. A macro index $j$ is *flagged* when <a id="eq:dyn:flag"></a>$$\log Q_{j+1}-\log Q_{j-N}>M,
 \label{eq:dyn:flag}$$ where the fixed threshold $M$ will be chosen sufficiently large below. Declare bad the union of the expanded flag intervals $[j-N,j+1+N)$ in the integer macro index line. Each bad macro is a single step. Divide every remaining macro interval into equal steps whose lengths are comparable to $\sqrt{S_j}$, for example by taking the number of pieces to be the nearest larger integer to $(S_{j+1}-S_j)/\sqrt{S_j}$. Once $j$ is large this gives <a href="#eq:dyn:grid-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:grid-size">[eq:dyn:grid-size]</a>.

<a id="eq:dyn:regular-rates"></a>
If macro $j$ is regular, it is not flagged. On each of its steps, <a href="#eq:dyn:rate-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:rate-input">[eq:dyn:rate-input]</a> and <a href="#eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order">[eq:dyn:endpoint-rate-order]</a> imply $$b_0Q_{j-N}\le\mu_i\le Q_{j+1},\qquad
 bQ_j\le\mu_i\le b^{-1}Q_j
 \label{eq:dyn:regular-rates}$$ for a fixed $b>0$ depending on $M,b_0$. These constants may depend on $h$ after all parameter choices have been made.

For a regular macro set $\theta_j=Q_j/j^2$ and choose bounded increasing multipliers $\lambda_j$ on the whole macro sequence by $$\lambda_{j+1}/\lambda_j=1+K_0/j^2.$$ Their product is bounded, since $\sum j^{-2}<\infty$; an overall constant factor is free. Choose $0<\epsilon_0<1/(10n)$. For each regular macro, form a finite directed graph whose vertices are the pairs $(k,i)$ consisting of a step in that macro and one of its coordinates. Give this vertex raw weight $\lambda_j(\mu_i-\theta_j)$, where $\mu_i$ is its rate in step $k$. Draw an edge to a vertex in the same or the next step precisely when the destination rate is at least the source rate minus $\epsilon_0\theta_j$. Define $w_{k,i}$ as the maximum raw weight among vertices from which $(k,i)$ is reachable, allowing paths of length zero. This maximum exists because the graph is finite, even when same-step edges form cycles. Assign the weight to both ends of its diagonal step.

<a id="eq:dyn:weights"></a>
This propagation loses at most $n\epsilon_0\theta_j$ in rates, independently of the number of steps. To verify the assertion, follow any permitted chain and keep its successive lower records. Once a sorted index has appeared, its rate at a later occurrence is at least its earlier rate, by <a href="#eq:dyn:loewner-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:loewner-jump">[eq:dyn:loewner-jump]</a>. A new lower record can therefore occur only on the first visit to an index; each such record lowers the previous record by at most $\epsilon_0\theta_j$. There are at most $n$ indices. It follows that $$\lambda_j(\mu_i-\theta_j)\le w_{k,i}
 \le\lambda_j(\mu_i-0.9\theta_j).
 \label{eq:dyn:weights}$$ In particular, whenever a permitted passage is possible the weights do not decrease.

The same nondecrease can be required across adjacent regular macros. Indeed, write $\mu'$ for a new rate and $\mu$ for an old one, with $\mu'\ge\mu-\epsilon_0\theta_j$. A lower bound on the new raw weight minus the upper bound on the old propagated weight is $$(\lambda_{j+1}-\lambda_j)\mu'
 -\lambda_{j+1}\theta_{j+1}
 +(0.9-\epsilon_0)\lambda_j\theta_j.$$ Since $\mu'\ge bQ_{j+1}$, choosing $K_0$ large in terms of $b$ makes this expression nonnegative at every sufficiently large index. Thus the passage rule is valid also at regular macro junctions. Increase the overall scale of $\lambda$ so that all weights are at least one after the eventual starting index.

<a id="eq:dyn:forbidden-gap"></a>
Call a nonlinear monomial $z^\beta$ in component $i$ *allowed* when $\sum_l\beta_lw_{k,l}\le w_{k,i}$, and *forbidden* otherwise. For a forbidden monomial of degree $m\ge2$, <a href="#eq:dyn:weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:weights">[eq:dyn:weights]</a> gives $$\sum_l\beta_l\mu_l-\mu_i
 >(0.9m-1)\theta_j\ge0.8\theta_j.
 \label{eq:dyn:forbidden-gap}$$ For an allowed monomial, every variable occurring in it has strictly smaller weight than its output: the weights are positive and $m\ge2$. Thus allowed nonlinear maps are triangular after ordering coordinates by weight.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Aligning the linear jumps

<a id="stmt-lem-linear-alignment"></a>
<div id="lem:linear-alignment" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:dyn:aligned-jump"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn:small-start-change"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Linear alignment). *On every run of regular steps there are unipotent matrices $L_k^+$, with off-diagonal entries only in rows of smaller weight than their columns, such that, on putting $L_k^-=D_k^{-1}L_k^+D_k$, the diagonal step derivative remains $D_k$ and every regular jump becomes $$J_k=L_{k+1}^-U_k(L_k^+)^{-1},\qquad
 (J_k)_{il}=0\quad\hbox{if }w_{k+1,i}<w_{k,l}.
 \label{eq:dyn:aligned-jump}$$ The matrices $L_k^+$ and their inverses have polynomial bounds in the macro index $j$, while $$\lVert L_k^- -I\rVert
 \le j^C\exp\left(-c\frac{\sqrt{S_j}}{j^2}\right).
 \label{eq:dyn:small-start-change}$$ The estimates are uniform in finite terminal horizons, and hold on an infinite regular run by a coefficientwise limit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-linear-alignment"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-linear-alignment</summary>

*Proof.* Work backward, taking $L_k^+=I$ at the last step of a finite run or horizon. Suppose the next small start adjustment is known and set $B=L_{k+1}^-U_k$. For a real threshold $x$, let $A_x$ be the span of rows of $B$ whose new weights are at most $x$, and let $E_x$ be the old coordinate row space with weights at most $x$.

Projection $P_{E_x}:A_x\to E_x$ is injective, and its inverse on its image has norm $O(j)$. For the proof assume $A_x\ne0$ and let $r_x$ be the largest new rate among its defining rows. Every old rate outside $E_x$ exceeds $r_x+\epsilon_0\theta_j$: otherwise the passage rule would make that old weight at most the weight of a selected new row. For any unit $a\in A_x$, <a href="#eq:dyn:loewner-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:loewner-jump">[eq:dyn:loewner-jump]</a> and the small next adjustment imply that its old rate Rayleigh quotient is at most $r_x+o(\theta_j)$. To see the size of this error, express $a$ as a combination of the selected rows of $L_{k+1}^-U_k$; its coefficient norm is $1+o(1)$, and the error is bounded by the operator norm of $L_{k+1}^- -I$ times the largest old or new rate. The regular rate bounds make that error $o(\theta_j)$, also at a macro junction. Nonnegativity of all old rates now gives $$(r_x+\epsilon_0\theta_j)
       (1-\lVert P_{E_x}a\rVert^2)
 \le r_x+o(\theta_j).$$ As $r_x\le C Q_j$, this implies $\lVert P_{E_x}a\rVert^2\ge c/j^2$. Empty or full coordinate spaces give the same assertion in its evident form.

We give the graph extension explicitly. List the distinct spaces $E_x$ increasingly. For each such space retain the largest required $A_x$ with that $E_x$, so that the required $A_x$ remain nested. Starting with the full row space, descend through the list. Suppose the next larger space $V_+$ already projects isomorphically to $E_+$ and contains the current $A=A_x$. Write $T_+:E_+\to V_+$ for its inverse projection, $E=E_x$, $Y=P_EA$, and $R_A:Y\to A$ for the inverse of $P_E|_A$. Define <a id="eq:dyn:graph-extension"></a>$$T_E(e)=R_A(P_Ye)+T_+((I-P_Y)e),\qquad e\in E.
 \label{eq:dyn:graph-extension}$$ Here orthogonal projection $P_Y$ is taken in $E$. Both terms lie in $V_+$, projection to $E$ is $e$, and the range contains $A$. Moreover $\lVert T_E\rVert\le\lVert R_A\rVert+\lVert T_+\rVert$. There are at most $n$ nontrivial stages, so the resulting graph lifts have polynomial bounds in $j$.

For each coordinate row, take its lift at its own weight threshold. These rows form $L_k^+$. The rows up to every threshold span the corresponding nested graph: they lie there and their projections form a triangular basis of its coordinate space. Each lifted row equals its coordinate row plus terms of strictly higher weight. Hence $L_k^+=I+N_k$ with $N_k$ nilpotent, and $(L_k^+)^{-1}=\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}(-N_k)^r$ has polynomial bounds. The inclusion $A_x(L_k^+)^{-1}\subset E_x$ is exactly the zero pattern in <a href="#eq:dyn:aligned-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:aligned-jump">[eq:dyn:aligned-jump]</a>.

For every nonzero off-diagonal entry in row $i$, column $l$, the within-step passage rule gives $\mu_l-\mu_i>\epsilon_0\theta_j$. Conjugation therefore gives $$(D_k^{-1}L_k^+D_k)_{il}
 =(L_k^+)_{il}\exp\bigl(- (\mu_l-\mu_i)\Delta\tau_k/2\bigr).$$ By <a href="#eq:dyn:regular-rates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:regular-rates">[eq:dyn:regular-rates]</a>, $\theta_j\Delta\tau_k\ge c\sqrt{S_j}/j^2$. This proves <a href="#eq:dyn:small-start-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:small-start-change">[eq:dyn:small-start-change]</a>; its exponential absorbs every polynomial bound needed in the preceding backward step. After a sufficiently late start the induction thus closes uniformly in the horizon. A diagonal subsequence of finite-horizon matrices proves the assertion on infinite runs; the zero conditions and all bounds are closed under that limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Homological equations and polynomial automorphisms

Choose a single primary coordinate system at each grid endpoint. Use the adjusted start coordinates if a regular step starts there; otherwise use the adjusted end coordinates of a regular step just ended, if there is one; use the volume coordinates in all other cases. These choices are a linear change, of polynomial size together with its inverse, following $A_k$. For a regular step, before the possible jump to the next regular start, write the resulting transition as $\widehat F_k$, with derivative $D_k$. Its actual transition to the next primary coordinates is $J_k\widehat F_k$, with $J_k=I$ when the next step is bad. It has a constant-volume $d$-jet and polynomially bounded coefficients. This follows by formal composition and inversion of the primary coordinate jets; only their local germs are used.

<a id="eq:dyn:homological-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn:homological-layer"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn:backward-contraction"></a>
We construct tangent-to-identity polynomial jets $h_k$ at regular starts, taking $h_k=I$ elsewhere. For a regular step seek $G_k=J_kg_k$, where $g_k$ has linear part $D_k$ and only allowed nonlinear terms, satisfying $$g_k\circ h_k
 =J_k^{-1}\circ h_{k+1}\circ J_k\circ\widehat F_k
 \quad\text{through degree }d.
 \label{eq:dyn:homological-identity}$$ In homogeneous degree $m$, once lower degrees have been fixed, this is $$g_k^{(m)}+D_kh_k^{(m)}
 =J_k^{-1}h_{k+1}^{(m)}(J_kD_kz)+B_k^{(m)}(z),
 \label{eq:dyn:homological-layer}$$ where $B_k^{(m)}$ contains only already known data. Put only forbidden coefficients into $h_k^{(m)}$ and only allowed coefficients into $g_k^{(m)}$. If $\Pi_k$ projects to the forbidden coefficients, the equation for $h_k^{(m)}$ is a backward affine recursion with linear operator <a id="eq:dyn:backward-operator"></a>$$T_kX=\Pi_k\left[D_k^{-1}J_k^{-1}X(J_kD_kz)\right].
 \label{eq:dyn:backward-operator}$$ Conjugation by $J_k$ has polynomial norm in $j$ at each fixed degree. After it has been expanded, a coefficient in output $i$ and input $\beta$ receives the diagonal factor $\exp[-(\sum_l\beta_l\mu_l-\mu_i)\Delta\tau_k/2]$. Projection and <a href="#eq:dyn:forbidden-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:forbidden-gap">[eq:dyn:forbidden-gap]</a> consequently give $$\lVert T_k\rVert\le j^{C_m}
       \exp\left(-c\frac{\sqrt{S_j}}{j^2}\right).
 \label{eq:dyn:backward-contraction}$$ This estimate holds for every incoming homogeneous polynomial $X$; no support restriction on $X$ has been imposed before conjugation.

Solve first on a finite regular horizon with terminal correction the identity, and proceed by degree. Inductively the lower-layer forcing, including division by $D_k$, has coefficients bounded by $\exp(C'_m\sqrt{s_k})$: regular steps have $\Delta s_k\asymp\sqrt{s_k}$, and $\lVert D_k^{-1}\rVert\le e^{\Delta s_k/2}$. The envelopes $\exp(C_m\sqrt{s_k})$ have bounded consecutive ratios, because $\sqrt{s_{k+1}}-\sqrt{s_k}$ is bounded on regular steps. Thus <a href="#eq:dyn:backward-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:backward-contraction">[eq:dyn:backward-contraction]</a> is eventually less than one half after weighting by these envelopes. The backward geometric series solves <a href="#eq:dyn:homological-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:homological-layer">[eq:dyn:homological-layer]</a> with <a id="eq:dyn:regular-coefficients"></a>$$\lVert h_k\rVert+\lVert J_dh_k^{-1}\rVert+\lVert g_k\rVert
       +\lVert g_k^{-1}\rVert_{\mathrm{coeff}}
 \le\exp(C_d'\sqrt{s_k}),
 \label{eq:dyn:regular-coefficients}$$ uniformly in the terminal horizon. Here $C_d'$ may depend on all fixed grid parameters. Taking coefficientwise diagonal limits gives the same construction on an infinite regular run.

Each $g_k$ is a triangular polynomial automorphism: all nonlinear inputs have smaller weights than their output. Its inverse is found by successive substitution in increasing weight order. Its degree and inverse degree are bounded in terms of $n,d$, and its determinant is the constant $\det D_k$.

The coordinate jets $h_k$ also preserve volume through degree $d-1$. This is not a property assumed of the forbidden projection. In a finite-horizon problem, differentiate the complete jet identity <a href="#eq:dyn:homological-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:homological-identity">[eq:dyn:homological-identity]</a>. The determinant of $\widehat F_k'$ has the same constant jet as $\det D_k$, and $g_k$ has that determinant exactly. If $\det h_{k+1}'=1$ through degree $d-1$, then the determinant identity forces the same statement for $h_k$. Backward induction from the identity terminal jet proves it, and it passes to the coefficientwise limits.

At a bad macro step realize, by Lemma <a href="#lem:volume-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:volume-jets">28</a>, the jet of the transition between the two primary coordinate systems after their endpoint corrections $h_k,h_{k+1}$. It is a constant-volume jet by what was just proved. Enlarge $D_d$ to bound the degrees and inverse degrees of these models and of the regular models. Let $H_k$ be the degree-$d$ truncation of the total primary change followed by $h_k$. It has the required center derivative and inverse jet, and <a href="#eq:dyn:jet-conjugacy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:jet-conjugacy">[eq:dyn:jet-conjugacy]</a> follows.

For precision, on a bad step the realized input jet has coefficient bound $\exp(o(s_k))$, whereas its inverse linear part is bounded by $\exp(\Delta s_k/2+o(s_k))$, by <a href="#eq:dyn:transition-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:transition-linear">[eq:dyn:transition-linear]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:volume-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:volume-jets">28</a> costs a fixed polynomial in these two quantities. Its polynomial exponent depends only on $n,d$. Therefore it gives <a href="#eq:dyn:coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:coefficient-bound">[eq:dyn:coefficient-bound]</a> with a coefficient $C_d$ independent of $h,N,M$. All endpoint and regular costs $\exp(C(d,h,N,M)\sqrt{s_k})$ remain in $\exp(o(s_k))$. Finally the linear part of the jet conjugacy is $G_k'(0)=H_{k+1}'(0)F_k'(0)H_k'(0)^{-1}$; the polynomial bounds on the endpoint linear changes give <a href="#eq:dyn:center-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:center-inverse">[eq:dyn:center-inverse]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Charging bad components to rate growth

<a id="stmt-lem-degree-control"></a>
<div id="lem:degree-control" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Forward degree control). *The flag threshold $M$ can be chosen so that regular macro starts occur arbitrarily late, and the polynomial models constructed above, initialized at any sufficiently late such start, satisfy <a href="#eq:dyn:forward-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:forward-degree">[eq:dyn:forward-degree]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-degree-control"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-degree-control</summary>

<a id="eq:dyn:flag-threshold"></a>
*Proof.* Set $$W=2N+1,\qquad D_*=D_d,\qquad
 B_* =\max\{0,\log(2/b_0)\},\qquad \alpha=\frac{M}{3W}.$$ Choose $M$ so large that $$\alpha\ge\log D_*+B_*/W,\qquad \alpha>h.
 \label{eq:dyn:flag-threshold}$$ A flag $f$ has expanded interval $I_f=[f-N,f+1+N)$, of length $W$, and growth interval $[f-N,f+1]$, on which $\log Q$ increases by more than $M$.

<a id="eq:dyn:bad-reset-cost"></a>
Consider a full finite bad component $[u,v)$. Choose a maximal disjoint collection of its expanded flag intervals. Their concentric triples cover the component: any unselected equal-length interval meets a selected one and is contained in its triple. Thus there are at least $(v-u)/(3W)$ selected intervals. Their growth intervals are disjoint up to endpoints and lie in $[u,v-N]$. Monotonicity gives <a id="eq:dyn:full-bad-growth"></a>$$\log\frac{Q_{v-N}}{Q_u}\ge\alpha(v-u).
 \label{eq:dyn:full-bad-growth}$$ Every full component has length $L=v-u\ge W$, so <a href="#eq:dyn:flag-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:flag-threshold">[eq:dyn:flag-threshold]</a> implies $$D_*^L Q_u\le e^{-B_*L/W}Q_{v-N}
             \le(b_0/2)Q_{v-N}.
 \label{eq:dyn:bad-reset-cost}$$

We now track the ordinary degree of each component of the accumulated forward polynomial. At a regular start bound these degrees by the respective weights. Allowed nonlinear terms preserve that bound under composition, and <a href="#eq:dyn:aligned-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:aligned-jump">[eq:dyn:aligned-jump]</a> preserves it at a regular jump. If the next macro is bad, retain the last end weights; at entry to $[u,v)$ their maximum is at most $\lambda_{u-1}Q_u$. Each bad map multiplies maximum degree by at most $D_*$. At the next regular macro $v$, its flag test fails, and hence $Q_v\le e^M Q_{v-N}$. For sufficiently large $v$ every new start weight is at least $$\lambda_v\bigl(b_0Q_{v-N}-Q_v/v^2\bigr)
 \ge\lambda_v(b_0/2)Q_{v-N}.$$ As $\lambda_v\ge\lambda_{u-1}$, <a href="#eq:dyn:bad-reset-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:bad-reset-cost">[eq:dyn:bad-reset-cost]</a> resets all degree bounds exactly to the new weights. There is no multiplicative loss for a completed bad component.

<a id="eq:dyn:bad-prefix-growth"></a>
Prefixes require a separate estimate. For a bad prefix $[u,j)$, the expanded flag intervals wholly contained in the prefix cover $[u,j-W)$, when this interval is nonempty. Indeed an expanded interval covering a point before $j-W$ ends before $j$, and none crosses the left endpoint $u$ of the component. The same disjoint selection gives $$\log(Q_j/Q_u)\ge\alpha(j-u-W)_+,
 \qquad D_*^{j-u}Q_u\le D_*^W Q_j.
 \label{eq:dyn:bad-prefix-growth}$$ This proof also applies to a prefix of an infinite bad component. Such a component would give $\log(Q_j/S_j)\ge(\alpha-h)j-O(1)\to\infty$. It is impossible: choose continuous-clock points $x_r\to\infty$ with $q(x_r)\le3x_r$, and let $S_{j_r}\le x_r<S_{j_r+1}$. Then $Q_{j_r}\le3e^h S_{j_r}$, contradicting that growth. Thus there are regular macro starts arbitrarily far out.

Start at one of them, after every fixed-parameter threshold above, and label this grid point $k=0$, retaining the original large macro indices $j$. The accumulated map is initially the identity and its component degrees are one, so the initial weight bound holds after the overall scaling already allowed for $\lambda$. Regular propagation, the exact full-component reset, and the single prefix loss in <a href="#eq:dyn:bad-prefix-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:bad-prefix-growth">[eq:dyn:bad-prefix-growth]</a> give, at all macro endpoints, $$\deg G_{0,k(j)}\le
 C\max\{e^M,D_*^W\}Q_j,$$ where $C$ can be taken to depend on the bounded supremum of the multipliers. On a regular macro one uses $Q_{j+1}\le e^M Q_j$; inside a bad component one uses the prefix estimate. Applying the bound at the endpoints $j_r$ just selected proves <a href="#eq:dyn:forward-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:forward-degree">[eq:dyn:forward-degree]</a>. This uses only a subsequence with $Q_j=O(S_j)$, never an all-time bound on $q(s)/s$. ◻

</details>

Lemmas <a href="#lem:volume-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:volume-jets">28</a>, <a href="#lem:linear-alignment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:linear-alignment">29</a>, and <a href="#lem:degree-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:degree-control">30</a>, together with the homological construction, complete the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:polynomial-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polynomial-models">27</a>.

<a id="sec:uniformization"></a>
### Convergence and surjectivity of the coordinates

We now pass from the polynomial models to a global map. The local errors must remain small under backward polynomial iteration, and the subsequential forward degree bound must then rule out a proper image. We give both steps with their domains and parameter choices.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### Parameter order and local error estimates

<a id="eq:unif:decay-parameters"></a>
<a id="eq:unif:jet-order"></a>
Decrease the common chart exponent $\sigma$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:shrinking-charts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shrinking-charts">26</a>, if necessary, so that $0<\sigma<1$. Choose $$0<\gamma<\gamma'<\min\{\sigma/2,1\},
 \qquad 0<10\nu<\min\{c\gamma,\sigma,\gamma,1\},
 \label{eq:unif:decay-parameters}$$ with $\gamma$ in the range of its small-ball transition estimate; decrease its constant $c$ to at most one. Fix the jet order so large that $$(d+1)\nu>30.
 \label{eq:unif:jet-order}$$ Only after this choice fix a sufficiently small $h>0$, as specified below. Then choose $N,M,b,K_0,\lambda$ as in Section <a href="#sec:dynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:dynamics">8</a>, and finally choose a sufficiently late regular macro start. The estimates in Proposition <a href="#prop:polynomial-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polynomial-models">27</a> hold for this choice.

On a fixed smaller raw transition ball, the jet conjugacy and Cauchy estimates give <a id="eq:unif:remainder"></a>$$\lvert H_{k+1}F_k(z)-G_kH_k(z)\rvert
 \le \exp\bigl(A_d\Delta s_k+o(s_k)\bigr)\lvert z\rvert^{d+1}.
 \label{eq:unif:remainder}$$ Here $A_d$ is independent of $h$. Indeed the degrees of the polynomials in this expression are fixed, the $F_k$ are uniformly bounded on a larger raw ball, and the coefficient estimates of Proposition <a href="#prop:polynomial-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polynomial-models">27</a> bound the two compositions there. They agree through degree $d$, so the Cauchy remainder has the displayed factor. This argument uses $H_{k+1}F_k-G_kH_k$ directly; it requires no uniform analytic domain for $H_k^{-1}$.

We shall use two sequences of raw chart coordinates:

1.  $z_k=f_{s_k}(x)$, uniformly for $x$ in any fixed compact neighborhood in the exhaustion and all sufficiently large $k$;

2.  $z_k=F_{s_a,s_k}(z)$, uniformly for $\lvert z\rvert\le e^{-\gamma s_a}$, for any sufficiently large $a$ and all $k\ge a$.

<a id="eq:unif:small-orbits"></a>
In the first case the threshold may depend on the compact; in the second it is uniform in $a$. Continuation and composition of the transition germs give $z_{k+1}=F_k(z_k)$ in both cases. The chart estimates and <a href="#eq:unif:decay-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:decay-parameters">[eq:unif:decay-parameters]</a> imply, eventually, $$\lvert z_k\rvert\le e^{-5\nu s_k},\qquad
 \zeta_k:=H_k(z_k),\quad
 \lvert\zeta_k\rvert\le e^{-2\nu s_k}.
 \label{eq:unif:small-orbits}$$ In regime (ii), the case $k=a$ uses $F_{s_a,s_a}=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$ and $\gamma>10\nu$.

<a id="eq:unif:macro-choice"></a>
<a id="eq:unif:orbit-defect"></a>
Choose $h$ so small that, with $\delta=e^h-1$, $$\delta<\tfrac1{10},\qquad
 A_d\delta<1,\qquad C_d\delta<\nu/4,
 \label{eq:unif:macro-choice}$$ enlarging $A_d,C_d$ first to cover the finitely many fixed-degree coefficient estimates used here. By <a href="#eq:unif:jet-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:jet-order">[eq:unif:jet-order]</a>–<a href="#eq:unif:small-orbits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:small-orbits">[eq:unif:small-orbits]</a>, after the late start the defect satisfies $$r_k:=\zeta_{k+1}-G_k(\zeta_k),\qquad
 \lvert r_k\rvert\le e^{-10s_k}.
 \label{eq:unif:orbit-defect}$$ All constants dependent on $h,N,M$ multiply $o(s_k)$ in this argument and are absorbed only by that late start. In particular, <a href="#eq:unif:macro-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:macro-choice">[eq:unif:macro-choice]</a> does not require choosing $h$ using a constant which is itself determined by $M$.

<a id="eq:unif:inverse-tube"></a>
The inverse maps have the local Lipschitz bound $$\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}\left(G_k^{-1}\bigm|_{B(e^{-\nu s_{k+1}})}\right)
 \le e^{\Delta s_k}
 \label{eq:unif:inverse-tube}$$ at all sufficiently large indices. To check it, the derivative of $G_k^{-1}$ on this ball is bounded by the sum of $$s_k^C e^{\Delta s_k/2}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 \exp\bigl(C_d\Delta s_k+o(s_k)-\nu s_{k+1}\bigr).$$ The first term is at most $e^{3\Delta s_k/4}$ eventually, since $\Delta s_k\ge c_h\sqrt{s_k}$. The second tends to zero by <a href="#eq:unif:macro-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:macro-choice">[eq:unif:macro-choice]</a>; their sum is at most $e^{\Delta s_k}$ after increasing the starting index. Integrating the derivative along segments in this convex ball gives <a href="#eq:unif:inverse-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:inverse-tube">[eq:unif:inverse-tube]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Backward approximation inside valid tubes

<a id="stmt-lem-unif-backward-limit"></a>
<div id="lem:unif:backward-limit" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:unif:shadow"></a>
**Lemma 31** (Convergence of corrected coordinates). *For either raw sequence above and every sufficiently large $k$, $$\xi_k=\lim_{l\to\infty}G_{k,l}^{-1}\zeta_l$$ exists locally uniformly in the indicated variables. It is holomorphic and satisfies $$\lvert\xi_k-\zeta_k\rvert\le e^{-6s_k},\qquad
 \xi_{k+1}=G_k\xi_k.
 \label{eq:unif:shadow}$$ All applications of <a href="#eq:unif:inverse-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:inverse-tube">[eq:unif:inverse-tube]</a> take place inside its stated balls.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-unif-backward-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-unif-backward-limit</summary>

*Proof.* For a terminal index $l$ put $y_l^{(l)}=\zeta_l$ and $y_i^{(l)}=G_i^{-1}y_{i+1}^{(l)}$ for $i<l$. Simultaneously establish, by backward induction, that the comparison segments at step $i$ lie in $B(e^{-\nu s_{i+1}})$ and that <a id="eq:unif:finite-error-sum"></a>$$\lvert y_k^{(l)}-\zeta_k\rvert
 \le \sum_{i=k}^{l-1}
       e^{-10s_i}\exp(s_{i+1}-s_k).
 \label{eq:unif:finite-error-sum}$$ The terminal assertion is immediate. At a backward step compare $G_i^{-1}y_{i+1}^{(l)}$ with $G_i^{-1}G_i\zeta_i=\zeta_i$. Both arguments are close to $\zeta_{i+1}$: the first by the inductive bound and the second by <a href="#eq:unif:orbit-defect" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:orbit-defect">[eq:unif:orbit-defect]</a>. Since $\lvert\zeta_{i+1}\rvert\le e^{-2\nu s_{i+1}}$, they and their joining segment lie in the required larger tube whenever the error sum has the bound asserted below. Applying <a href="#eq:unif:inverse-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:inverse-tube">[eq:unif:inverse-tube]</a> then gives the next error sum.

Indeed $s_{i+1}\le(1+\delta)s_i$ and the grid eventually has spacing at least one, so $$\sum_{i\ge k}e^{-10s_i+s_{i+1}-s_k}
 \le e^{-s_k}\sum_{i\ge k}e^{-(9-\delta)s_i}
 \le C e^{-(10-\delta)s_k}\le e^{-6s_k}.$$ As $\nu<1/10$, this is much smaller than the difference between the two tube radii. The estimates therefore close the simultaneous induction without evaluating an inverse outside its proved domain.

For completeness, consecutive terminal approximations are Cauchy by the same reasoning. At level $l$ their difference is at most $e^{\Delta s_l}e^{-10s_l}$, and propagation down to $k$ bounds it by $e^{-10s_l+s_{l+1}-s_k}$. These bounds are summable in $l$. They are uniform on the compact sets in either regime, so the limits are holomorphic. The finite identities give $\xi_{k+1}=G_k\xi_k$, and the infinite error sum proves <a href="#eq:unif:shadow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:shadow">[eq:unif:shadow]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
#### A coherent injective map on the manifold

<a id="stmt-prop-global-coordinates"></a>
<div id="prop:global-coordinates" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:unif:global-map"></a>
<a id="eq:unif:forward-smallness"></a>
<a id="eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion"></a>
**Proposition 32** (Global coordinates). *The locally uniform limit on the exhaustion $$\Psi(x)=\lim_{l\to\infty}G_{0,l}^{-1}H_lf_{s_l}(x)
 \label{eq:unif:global-map}$$ defines an injective holomorphic map $M\to\mathbb C^n$ with $\Psi(o)=0$. Its image $\Omega$ is open, and $\Psi:M\to\Omega$ is a biholomorphism. Moreover, on every compact $K\subset\Omega$, $$\sup_K\lvert G_{0,k}\rvert\le e^{-(\sigma/2)s_k}
 \quad\hbox{eventually},
 \label{eq:unif:forward-smallness}$$ and, at every sufficiently large $k$, $$G_{0,k}^{-1}\bigl(B(e^{-\gamma's_k})\bigr)\subset\Omega.
 \label{eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-global-coordinates"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-global-coordinates</summary>

*Proof.* On a fixed exhaustion compact choose an index $k$ after which regime (i) is available. Lemma <a href="#lem:unif:backward-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:unif:backward-limit">31</a> gives $\xi_k$ there, and composing it with the single fixed polynomial automorphism $G_{0,k}^{-1}$ gives the limit <a href="#eq:unif:global-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:global-map">[eq:unif:global-map]</a>. On overlaps the inverse charts agree by continuation, and <a href="#eq:unif:shadow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:shadow">[eq:unif:shadow]</a> makes the limits agree. Thus these maps define a holomorphic $\Psi$ on all of $M$. All maps fix zero in charts, so $\Psi(o)=0$.

Suppose $\Psi(x)=\Psi(y)$ for two fixed points. For every large $k$, $G_{0,k}\Psi=\xi_k$ on a compact containing them, and hence <a href="#eq:unif:shadow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:shadow">[eq:unif:shadow]</a> implies $$\lvert H_kf_{s_k}(x)-H_kf_{s_k}(y)\rvert\le2e^{-6s_k}.$$ On the tiny raw-coordinate ball containing these points, write $H_k(z)=A_kz+E_k(z)$, where $A_k=H_k'(0)$ has conorm at least $s_k^{-C}$ and $\lVert DE_k\rVert$ is bounded by $\exp(o(s_k))$ times the radius. Integrating only $DE_k$ along the segment and retaining the fixed linear term $A_k$ gives $$|H_k(z)-H_k(w)|\ge
 \bigl(s_k^{-C}-\sup\lVert DE_k\rVert\bigr)|z-w|.$$ The nonlinear error is eventually less than $s_k^{-C}/2$, so <a id="eq:unif:raw-separation"></a>$$\lvert f_{s_k}(x)-f_{s_k}(y)\rvert\le e^{-5s_k}
 \label{eq:unif:raw-separation}$$ eventually.

If $x\ne y$, choose a small relatively compact initial metric ball about $x$ whose closure excludes $y$. Map the straight segment between the two raw coordinates by $\Phi_{s_k}$, obtaining a path from $x$ to $y$. The segment stays in the uniform chart ball. Up to its first exit from the fixed ball about $x$, the compact metric comparison $e^{-2s_k}g\le h_{t(s_k)}$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:ricci-windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:ricci-windows">23</a>, and the uniform chart upper bound $\Phi_{s_k}^*h_{t(s_k)}\le Cg_{\mathrm{Eucl}}$, bound its initial-metric length by $Ce^{s_k}e^{-5s_k}$. This tends to zero and cannot reach the boundary of that fixed ball. The contradiction proves injectivity. This argument uses immersed charts only on their indicated sheet; it requires no global injectivity of $\Phi_{s_k}$.

Nonsingularity follows directly from the same quantitative estimates. Fix a point and two coordinate neighborhoods $U\Subset V$ whose closures lie in one compact exhaustion set. The estimates of regime (i) hold uniformly on $\overline V$ for every sufficiently large $k$. Differentiating $\Phi_{s_k}f_{s_k}=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$, and using <a href="#chart:metric-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="chart:metric-decay">[chart:metric-decay]</a> and <a href="#chart:uniform-chart-metric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="chart:uniform-chart-metric">[chart:uniform-chart-metric]</a>, gives $$|df_{s_k}(v)|\ge c e^{-s_k}|v|_g
 \quad\text{on }\overline V.$$ The coefficient bounds on $H_k$, its polynomially bounded inverse linear part, and $|f_{s_k}|\le e^{-\sigma s_k}$ therefore imply $$|d(H_kf_{s_k})(v)|\ge c s_k^{-C}e^{-s_k}|v|_g.$$ On the other hand, the shadow bound $|\xi_k-H_kf_{s_k}|\le e^{-6s_k}$ on $\overline V$ and Cauchy’s estimate on the fixed pair $U\Subset V$ bound its differential on $U$ by $C e^{-6s_k}|v|_g$. Thus $$|d\xi_k(v)|\ge
 \bigl(c s_k^{-C}e^{-s_k}-C e^{-6s_k}\bigr)|v|_g>0
 \qquad(v\ne0)$$ for all sufficiently large $k$. Since $\xi_k=G_{0,k}\Psi$ and $G_{0,k}$ is a polynomial automorphism, $d\Psi$ is nonsingular. The holomorphic inverse theorem now makes the image $\Omega$ open; the local inverses agree by the proved injectivity, giving a holomorphic inverse on $\Omega$. Consequently $K'=\Psi^{-1}(K)$ is compact whenever $K\subset\Omega$ is compact. On $K'$, the common chart bound $\lvert f_{s_k}\rvert\le e^{-\sigma s_k}$, the coefficient bound on $H_k$, and <a href="#eq:unif:shadow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:shadow">[eq:unif:shadow]</a> give $$\sup_K\lvert G_{0,k}\rvert
 \le \exp\bigl(-\sigma s_k+o(s_k)\bigr)+e^{-6s_k},$$ which proves <a href="#eq:unif:forward-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:forward-smallness">[eq:unif:forward-smallness]</a>.

It remains to prove the ball inclusion. Fix a sufficiently large $k$ and use regime (ii) with $a=k$. For large $l$, the inverse chart $f_{s_l}$ is defined on the compact image under $\Phi_{s_k}$ of the closed ball $\overline B(e^{-\gamma s_k})$. The identity $f_{s_l}\Phi_{s_k}=F_{s_k,s_l}$ holds first as a germ at zero and then throughout that ball by holomorphic continuation. Therefore Lemma <a href="#lem:unif:backward-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:unif:backward-limit">31</a> gives <a id="eq:unif:chart-shadow"></a>$$\sup_{\lvert z\rvert\le e^{-\gamma s_k}}
 \lvert G_{0,k}\Psi\Phi_{s_k}(z)-H_k(z)\rvert
 \le e^{-6s_k}.
 \label{eq:unif:chart-shadow}$$ Write $A=H_k'(0)$, $r=e^{-\gamma s_k}$, and $$T_k=G_{0,k}\Psi\Phi_{s_k},\qquad E_k(z)=T_k(z)-Az.$$ The map $T_k$ is holomorphic on the fixed chart ball, and fixes zero. Its shadow estimate and the coefficient bound on $H_k$ give $$E_k(0)=0,\qquad \lVert A^{-1}\rVert\le s_k^C,\qquad
 \sup_{\lvert z\rvert\le r}|E_k(z)|
 \le \exp(o(s_k))r^2+e^{-6s_k}.$$ Cauchy’s estimate on the smaller ball then gives $$\sup_{\lvert z\rvert\le r/2}\lVert A^{-1}DE_k(z)\rVert
 \le C_n s_k^C\bigl(\exp(o(s_k))r+e^{-6s_k}/r\bigr)
 \mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,space=false,pdfliteral=direct,ActualText=27F6}%
  \longrightarrow%
  \EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}%
}0.$$ For large $k$ this supremum is at most $1/4$. Moreover, $\gamma'>\gamma$ implies $|A^{-1}w|\le r/4$ whenever $|w|\le e^{-\gamma's_k}$, after increasing the starting index once more. For such a target $w$, the map $$z\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,space=false,pdfliteral=direct,ActualText=27FC}%
  {\let\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,space=false,pdfliteral=direct,ActualText=27F6}%
  \longrightarrow%
  \EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}%
}\longrightarrow\longmapsto}%
  \EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}%
}A^{-1}w-A^{-1}E_k(z)$$ is $1/4$-Lipschitz on $\overline B(r/2)$ and maps this closed ball into $B(3r/8)$, because $E_k(0)=0$. Its successive iterates are Cauchy, stay in the closed ball, and converge to a fixed point $z$. The fixed-point identity is $T_k(z)=w$. Therefore $B(e^{-\gamma's_k})\subset G_{0,k}(\Omega)$, which is precisely <a href="#eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion">[eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:surjectivity"></a>
#### The image is all of affine space

<a id="eq:unif:polynomial-growth"></a>
We first record the polynomial growth estimate used in the final step. For a vector polynomial $P$ of degree at most $m$ and $0<R_1<R_2$, $$\sup_{B(R_2)}\lvert P\rvert
 \le (R_2/R_1)^m\sup_{B(R_1)}\lvert P\rvert.
 \label{eq:unif:polynomial-growth}$$ To verify this, restrict each scalar dual pairing of $P$ to a complex line through zero. The maximum principle in the exterior of the radius-$R_1$ disc, applied to the polynomial divided by $z^m$ including its removable value at infinity, gives the scalar bound. Taking the supremum over unit dual vectors and lines proves <a href="#eq:unif:polynomial-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:polynomial-growth">[eq:unif:polynomial-growth]</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-global-coordinates-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-global-coordinates</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:uniformization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:uniformization">1</a>.* The preceding analytic and chart constructions, followed by Propositions <a href="#prop:polynomial-models" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:polynomial-models">27</a> and <a href="#prop:global-coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:global-coordinates">32</a>, give a biholomorphism $\Psi:M\to\Omega$ onto an open set containing zero, with the forward smallness and inverse-ball inclusion proved above. It remains to show that $\Omega=\mathbb C^n$.

<a id="eq:unif:radii-choice"></a>
Suppose $R_*=\sup\{R>0:B(R)\subset\Omega\}<\infty$. It is positive because $0\in\Omega$ and $\Omega$ is open. By <a href="#eq:dyn:forward-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn:forward-degree">[eq:dyn:forward-degree]</a> there is an unbounded subsequence with $\deg G_{0,k}\le Ds_k$ for some finite $D$. Choose $0<R_1<R_*<R_2$ so close to $R_*$ that $$D\log(R_2/R_1)<\sigma/2-\gamma'.
 \label{eq:unif:radii-choice}$$ The closed smaller ball lies compactly in $\Omega$: it lies inside some still larger centered ball of radius below $R_*$. By <a href="#eq:unif:forward-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:forward-smallness">[eq:unif:forward-smallness]</a>, <a href="#eq:unif:polynomial-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:polynomial-growth">[eq:unif:polynomial-growth]</a>, and <a href="#eq:unif:radii-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:radii-choice">[eq:unif:radii-choice]</a>, the chosen subsequence satisfies $$\sup_{B(R_2)}\lvert G_{0,k}\rvert
 \le \exp\left[
   -\frac{\sigma}{2}s_k+Ds_k\log(R_2/R_1)\right]
 < e^{-\gamma's_k}$$ for all sufficiently large indices. Equation <a href="#eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion">[eq:unif:inverse-ball-inclusion]</a> then implies $B(R_2)\subset\Omega$, contradicting the definition of $R_*$. Therefore $R_*=\infty$, and $\Omega=\mathbb C^n$.

The map $\Psi$ in <a href="#eq:unif:global-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unif:global-map">[eq:unif:global-map]</a> is consequently a biholomorphism $M\to\mathbb C^n$. ◻

</details>

The surjectivity step used the degrees of the forward compositions $G_{0,k}$ on their available subsequence; no degree estimate for their inverses, or uniform instantaneous contraction ratio, is required.

Their later paper credits ChatGPT 5.5 Pro with the auxiliary function and induction for the Bézout estimates, and ChatGPT 5.0 in thinking mode with the heat-comparison estimate recalled as Lemma 2.2 . Wu reports ChatGPT-6 assistance in developing the proofs and the author’s verification of the arguments .
